Compressible Euler limit from Boltzmann equation with Maxwell reflection boundary condition in half-space Thanks: ∗ Corresponding author August 8, 2026
Ning Jiang, Yi-Long Luo ∗, Shaojun Tang

Abstract
. Starting from the local-in-time classical solution to the compressible Euler system with impermeable boundary condition in half-space, by employing the coupled weak viscous layers (governed by linearized compressible Prandtl equations with Robin boundary condition) and linear kinetic boundary layers, and the analytical tools in [ 22 ] and some new boundary estimates both for Prandtl and Knudsen layers, we proved the local-in-time existence of Hilbert expansion type classical solutions to the scaled Boltzmann equation with Maxwell reflection boundary condition with accommodation coefficient α ε = O ⁡ ( ε ) \alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}) when the Knudsen number ε \varepsilon small enough. As a consequence, this justifies the corresponding case of formal analysis in Sone’s books [ 37 , 38 ] . This also extends the results in [ 23 ] from specular to Maxwell reflection boundary condition. Both of this paper and [ 23 ] can be viewed as generalizations of Caflisch’s classic work [ 9 ] to the cases with boundary. Keywords. Compressible Euler limit; Boltzmann equation; Maxwell reflection boundary condition; Accommodation coefficients; Hilbert expansion. AMS subject classifications. 35B25; 35F20; 35Q20; 76N15; 82C40



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Boltzmann equation with Maxwell boundary condition

Hydrodynamic limits from the Boltzmann equation has been one of the central problems in fluid dynamics and kinetic theory since late 1970’s. Among many references, we list some standard books for thorough introduction to the Boltzmann equation and fluid limits, for example,
[
7
,
8
,
34
]
. Despite the tremendous progress in this fields (which we will review later), the most important problem basically remains open: the limit from the Boltzmann equation to the compressible Euler system.

More precisely, the Boltzmann equation in the Euler scaling is the following form:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}F_{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}B(F_{\varepsilon},F_{\varepsilon})\quad\qquad{\text{on}}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\\[4.2679pt]
F_{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=F_{\varepsilon}^{in}(x,v)\geq 0\qquad\ \,\qquad{\text{on}}\ \mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\end{array}\right.

where the dimensionless number
\varepsilon>0
is the Knudsen number which is the ratio of mean free path and macroscopic length scale, and
F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\geq 0
is the density of particles of velocity
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and position
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3};x_{3}>0\}
, a half-space. Moreover, the equation (
1.1
) is imposed with the Maxwellian reflection boundary condition

(1.2)

\gamma_{-}F_{\varepsilon}=(1-\alpha_{\varepsilon})L\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}+\alpha_{\varepsilon}K\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}\quad\textrm{on }\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Sigma_{-}\,,

For the derivation from the dimensional Boltzamnn equation to the scaled form (
1.1
), the readers can find the details in the aforementioned books, or papers
[
2
,
3
]
.

The Boltzmann collision operator is defined as

(1.3)

\displaystyle B(F_{1},F_{2})(v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|v-u|^{\gamma_{0}}F_{1}(u^{\prime})F_{2}(v^{\prime})b(\theta)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|v-u|^{\gamma_{0}}F_{1}(u)F_{2}(v)b(\theta)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}u\,,

where
u^{\prime}=u+[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\omega
,
v^{\prime}=u+[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\omega
,
\cos\theta=(v-u)\cdot\omega/|v-u|
,
0<b(\theta)\leq C|\cos\theta|
, and
0\leq\gamma_{0}\leq 1
means the hard potential. Such collision operators cover the hard-sphere interaction with an angular cutoff. The accommodation coefficient
\alpha_{\varepsilon}\in[0,1]
describes how much the molecules accommodate to the state of the wall. The special case
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=0
corresponds to specular reflection, while
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=1
refers to complete diffusion. Usually this coefficient can be taken the form
\chi\varepsilon^{\beta}
with
\beta\geq 0
. In this paper, we consider the form
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Analytically, the cases
\beta\neq\frac{1}{2},0
will be similar to the current paper (but with more complicated expansion of boundary layers). For the simplicity of the presentation, we will write it in an separate forthcoming paper. For the case
\beta=0
, the formal analysis will be the same (all the details are in Sone’s books
[
37
,
38
]
), except the leading order involves
nonlinear
compressible Prandtl equation, whose well-posedness (even local) in Sobolev type space is completely open.

We denote
n=(0,0,-1)
by the outward normal of
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. Let
\Sigma:=\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the phase space boundary of
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The phase boundary
\Sigma
can be split by outgoing boundary
\Sigma_{+}
, incoming boundary
\Sigma_{-}
, and grazing boundary
\Sigma_{0}
:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{+}=\{(x,v):x\in\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\,,v\cdot n=-v_{3}>0\}\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{-}=\{(x,v):x\in\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\,,v\cdot n=-v_{3}<0\}\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}=\{(x,v):x\in\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\,,v\cdot n=-v_{3}=0\}\,.

Let
\gamma_{\pm}F=\mathbbm{1}_{\Sigma_{\pm}}F
. The specular-reflection
L\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}
and the diffuse-reflection part
K\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.2
) are

\displaystyle L\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,R_{x}v)\,,\ R_{x}v=v-2(v\cdot n)n=(\bar{v},-v_{3})\,,

\displaystyle K\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\pi}M_{w}(t,\bar{x},v)\int_{v\cdot n>0}\gamma_{+}F_{\varepsilon}(v\cdot n)\mathrm{d}v\,,

respectively, where
M_{w}(t,\bar{x},v)
is the local Maxwellian distribution function corresponding to the wall (boundary) with the form

(1.4)

\displaystyle M_{w}(t,\bar{x},v)=\frac{\rho_{w}(t,\bar{x})}{[2\pi T_{w}(t,\bar{x})]^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\Big\{-\frac{|v-u_{w}(t,\bar{x})|^{2}}{2T_{w}(t,\bar{x})}\Big\}\,.

The
\rho_{w},u_{w},T_{w}
are, respectively, density, velocity and temperature of the boundary. We also assume

u_{w,3}=0\,,

which denotes the boundary wall is fixed. We remark that the wall Maxwellian
M_{w}
in (
1.4
) plays a key role in this paper. It matches with the leading local Maxwellian from interior, see (
1.71
), which gives the compatibility condition between the initial and boundary data. It is one of the main difference or difficulty of the current paper with
[
23
]
in which the wall Maxwellian did not appear.

1.2.
History of compressible Euler limits

There have been many significant progress in the limits to incompressible fluids, such as Navier-Stokes, Stokes, or even Euler. We only list some representative results. One group of results is in the framework of DiPerna-Lions renormalized solutions of the Boltzmann equation
[
13
]
, i.e., the so-called BGL program, which aimed to justify the limit to Leray solutions of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. This was initialized by Bardos-Golse-Levermore
[
2
,
3
]
, and finished by Golse and Saint-Raymond
[
16
,
17
]
. The corresponding results in bounded domain were carried out in
[
30
,
27
]
. We also should mention the incompressible Euler limits of Saint-Raymond
[
32
,
33
]
. Another group of results is in the framework of classical solutions, which are based on nonlinear energy method, semi-group method or hypercoercivity (the latter two further rely on the spectral analysis of the linearized Boltzmann operator), see
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
14
,
19
,
25
,
28
]
.

Comparing to the incompressible limits listed above, the limit to the compressible Euler system from the Boltzmann equation is much limited. This is mainly due to our still very poor understanding of the well-posedness of compressible Euler system for which we do not even know how to define weak solutions (at least for multiple spatial dimensions). The only available global-in-time solution is the calibrated BV solution of Glimm in 1965
[
18
]
. Regarding the higher dimensions, there are local-in-time classical solutions (see standard textbook
[
29
]
). The compressible Euler limit from the Boltzmann equations dates back to Japanese school for analytical data and Caflisch’s Hilbert expansion approach which are based on the local well-posedness of compressible Euler system, see
[
9
,
31
]
. Later improvement of Caflisch result employing the recent progress on the
L^{2}\mbox{-}L^{\infty}
estimate was also used to help justifying the linearized acoustic limit
[
21
,
22
]
. We should also mention the compressible Euler limit in the context of 1-D Riemann problem away from the initial time in
[
24
]
.

In the domain with boundary, the situation is much more complicated. As formally (and numerically) analyzed by Japanese school (summarized in Sone’s books
[
37
,
38
]
), to derive the equations of compressible Euler system from the Boltzmann equations with Maxwell reflection condition (with non-zero accommodation coefficient) using Hilbert type expansion, there needed two coupled boundary layers: viscous and kinetic layers. The formal is also called
Prandtl layer
, the latter is
Knudsen layer
in the physics literatures. The reason that these two types of boundary layers are needed can be explained as follows: as well-known, the leading order in
interior
is compressible Euler system whose natural boundary condition is impermeable condition
\mathfrak{u}\!\cdot\!n=0
. However, the local Maxwellian governed by Euler system with this condition does not satisfy the Maxwell reflection boundary condition, except the specular reflection case (i.e.,
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=0
). So, kinetic layers with thickness
\varepsilon
are needed. In these layers, each term satisfies the linear kinetic boundary layer equations which requires
four
solvability conditions (by theorem of Golse-Perthame-Sulem
[
15
]
), but the number of boundary conditions for compressible Euler system is
one
. This mismatch indicates there should be another layer with thickness
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
: Prandtl layer. This name comes from the fact that in this layer, each term satisfies the famous Prandtl equations of compressible type. Specifically, if
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(1)
the leading term satisfies the
nonlinear
compressible Prandtl equations, the higher order terms satisfied the
linearized
compressible Prandtl equations. If
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon^{\beta})
with
\beta>0
, the boundary layers are weak, thus all the boundary terms appear in higher order. Thus, nonlinear Prandtl equation does not appear. The current paper aims to give a rigorous justification for the case
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.

In
[
23
]
, Guo-Huang-Wang started to justify the compressible Euler limit using Hilbert expansion approach, formally derived in Sone’s books. They considered the simplest case
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=0
, i.e., the specular reflection. We emphasize that in this case, the local Maxwellians governed by compressible Euler system with impermeable condition indeed satisfy the specular reflection condition. In this sense, boundary layers seem not needed. However, if the Hilbert expansion is used, at higher orders, the boundary conditions do not match. As a consequence, both fluid and kinetic layers are needed.

In the context of fluid equations, the application of the compressible Prandtl equations is not new. It has been investigated in the vanishing viscosity limit of compressible Naver-Stokes equations. This phenomena is consistent with paper
[
23
]
and the current paper, because as well-known, the compressible Navier-Stokes system with viscosity and thermal conductivity of order
O(\varepsilon)
can be derived from the Boltzmann equation (more precisely, by Chapman-Enskog expansion). This means
[
23
]
and current paper include this limit, but with different boundary conditions for compressible Navier-Stokes: paper
[
23
]
corresponds to Neumann boundary, the current paper corresponds to mixed Robin boundary. Indeed, at fluid equations level, Xin-Yanagisawa
[
39
]
proved the zero viscosity limit of linearized compressible Navier-Stokes system with Dirichlet condition, in which the linearized compressible Prandtl equations were also used. This result was extended to the case including temperature equation by Ding and the first author of the current paper in
[
12
]
.

We would like to mention that the analysis of coupled viscous and kinetic layers was already used in the work of the first author of this paper with Masmoudi
[
27
]
in slightly different form with
[
23
]
and this paper. The paper
[
27
]
is about the incompressible Navier-Stokes limit, which in fact does not need viscous layer. However, the major issue of
[
27
]
is to demonstrate the role played by acoustic waves, which is compressible. Thus, the incompressible Navier-Stokes limit (which happens in the time scale
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
) includes the acoustic limit in short time scale
O(1)
. In this sense, the two boundary layers in
[
27
]
are the same as the current papers and
[
23
]
(again, we emphasize that all the layers in
[
23
]
and here are linear because they appear in higher order terms). The main difference is that
[
27
]
works in the framework of renormalized solutions, which can not be expanded. So the coupled viscous and kinetic layers are considered in
dual
form in the sense that these layers appear in test functions. More specifically, the kinetic layers equations are the same as here and
[
23
]
, but with different boundary conditions: in this paper, for Maxwell reflection, the wall Maxwellian is local and nontrivial, while in
[
27
]
, the wall Maxwellian is global, i.e.,
M(v)
. In
[
23
]
, it is specular reflection, so there is no wall Maxwellian. For the viscous layer parts, in
[
27
]
, the stationary version, i.e., degenerate heat operators are used, while in
[
23
]
and current paper, the linearized compressible Prandtl equations are used. The more detailed technical difference between
[
23
]
and the current paper will be explained in Subsection
1.5
.

1.3.
Hilbert expansion

Throughout this paper, we use the notation
\bar{U}=(U_{1},U_{2})
for any vector
U=(U_{1},U_{2},U_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover, for simplicity of presentations, we use the notation
V^{0}:=V|_{x_{3}=0}
for any symbol
V=V(x)
, which may be a function, vector or operator. For any derivative operator
D_{x}
, we denote by
D_{x}V^{0}=(D_{x}V)^{0}\,.

For the functional spaces,
H^{s}
denotes the Sobolev space
W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
with norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
,
\|\cdot\|_{2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
are the
L^{2}
-norm and
L^{\infty}
-norm in both
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
variables, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the
L^{2}
-inner product.

For any function
G=G(t,\bar{x},y,v)
, with
(t,\bar{x},y,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the Taylor expansion at
y=0
is

(1.5)

G=G^{0}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq N}\tfrac{y^{l}}{l!}G^{(l)}+\tfrac{y^{N+1}}{(N+1)!}\widetilde{G}^{(N+1)}\,,

where the symbols

\displaystyle G^{(l)}=(\partial_{y}^{l}G)(t,\bar{x},0,v)\,,\ \widetilde{G}^{(N+1)}=(\partial_{y}^{N+1}G)(t,\bar{x},\eta,v)\ \textrm{for some }\eta\in(0,y).

In this paper, we take the Hilbert expansion approach to rigorously justify the asymptotic behaviors of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) as
\varepsilon\to 0
. As we already mentioned above, its corresponding formal analysis has basically been down in Sone’s books
[
37
,
38
]
. Analytically, the key of this approach is the estimate on the
remainder
, after suitable truncation.

Due to the thickness of viscous boundary layer is
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and the accommodation coefficient
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, we expand
F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
by order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. We remark that if
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon^{\beta})
with
\beta\neq\frac{1}{2},0
, the expansion will be more involved. This will be discussed in a separate forthcoming paper.

1.3.1.
Interior expansion

The expansion in interior has the form

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\thicksim\sum_{k\geq 0}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}F_{k}(t,x,v)\,.

Plugging into (
1.1
) and collecting the same orders,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-2}:

\displaystyle 0=B(F_{0},F_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1}:

\displaystyle 0=B(F_{0},F_{1})+B(F_{1},F_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0}:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})F_{0}=B(F_{0},F_{2})+B(F_{2},F_{0})+B(F_{1},F_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1}:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})F_{1}=B(F_{0},F_{3})+B(F_{3},F_{0})+B(F_{1},F_{2})+B(F_{2},F_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})F_{k}=B(F_{0},F_{k+2})+B(F_{k+2},F_{0})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k+2\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}B(F_{i},F_{j})\,.

Then
H
-theorem implies that
F_{0}
must be a local Maxwellian:

(1.7)

F_{0}(t,x,v):=\mathfrak{M}(t,x,v)=\frac{\rho(t,x)}{(2\pi T(t,x))^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\Big(-\frac{|v-\mathfrak{u}(t,x)|^{2}}{2T(t,x)}\Big)\,.

Here
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
represent the macroscopic density, bulk velocity and temperature, respectively. It is well-known that (see
[
38
]
, for instance), as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the solutions
F_{\varepsilon}
of the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) converge to a local Maxwellian
\mathfrak{M}
whose parameters
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
satisfy the compressible Euler system

(1.8)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho\mathfrak{u})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho\mathfrak{u})+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathfrak{u})+\nabla p=0\,,\\
&\partial_{t}\big[\rho\big(\tfrac{3}{2}T+\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\big)\big]+\mathrm{div}_{x}\big[\rho\mathfrak{u}\big(\tfrac{3}{2}T+\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\big)\big]+\mathrm{div}_{x}(p\mathfrak{u})=0\end{aligned}\right.

over
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
with the slip boundary condition

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}\cdot n|_{x_{3}=0}=-\mathfrak{u}_{3}|_{x_{3}=0}=-\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{0}=0\,,

where
p=\rho T
is the pressure. We further impose the initial data (this can be realized by setting special form of the initial data of the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
)):

(1.10)

\displaystyle(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)(0,x)=(\rho^{in},\mathfrak{u}^{in},T^{in})(x)

with compatibility condition

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}^{in}\cdot n|_{x_{3}}=0\,.

By
[
35
]
or
[
11
]
, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 1.1
.

Let
s_{0}\geq 3
and
\rho_{\#},T_{\#}>0
with
\rho_{\#}\sqrt{T_{\#}}<\frac{1}{3\sqrt{3}}
. Assume
(\rho^{in}-\rho_{\#},\mathfrak{u}^{in},T^{in}-T_{\#})\in H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
satisfies
0<\tfrac{3}{4}\rho_{\#}\leq\rho^{in}(x)\leq\tfrac{5}{4}\rho_{\#}\,,\quad 0<\tfrac{3}{4}T_{\#}\leq T^{in}(x)\leq\tfrac{5}{4}T_{\#}
. Then there is a
\tau>0
such that the compressible Euler system (
1.8
)-(
1.9
)-(
1.10
) admits a unique solution
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
such that

(\rho-\rho_{\#},\mathfrak{u},T-T_{\#})\in C\big([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\big)\cap C^{1}\big([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\big)

and

0<\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{\#}\leq\rho(t,x)\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\rho_{\#}\,,\quad 0<\tfrac{1}{2}T_{\#}\leq T(t,x)\leq\tfrac{3}{2}T_{\#}

hold for any
(t,x)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. Moreover, the following estimate holds:

(1.11)

\displaystyle\|(\rho-\rho_{\#},\mathfrak{u},T-T_{\#})\|_{C\big([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\big)\cap C^{1}\big([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\big)}\leq C_{0}\,.

Here the constants
\tau
,
C_{0}>0
depend only on the
H^{s_{0}}
-norm of
(\rho^{in}-\rho_{\#},\mathfrak{u}^{in},T^{in}-T_{\#})
.

By using the local Maxwellian
\mathfrak{M}(t,x,v)
, the linearized collision operator
\mathcal{L}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}g=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\Big\{B(\mathfrak{M},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}g)+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}g,\mathfrak{M})\Big\}\,.

The null space
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{L}
is spanned by (see
[
9
]
, for instance)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,,\ \tfrac{v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{\sqrt{\rho T}}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\ (i=1,2,3)\,,\ \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\left\{\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-3\right\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,.

The weighted
L^{2}
-norm

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}_{\nu}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|g(x,v)|^{2}\nu(v)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}v\,,

is defined by the collision frequency
\nu(v)\equiv\nu(\mathfrak{M})(v)

\displaystyle\nu(\mathfrak{M})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\theta)|v-v^{\prime}|^{\gamma_{0}}\mathfrak{M}(v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\omega\,.

Note that for given
0\leq\gamma_{0}\leq 1
,

(1.12)

\displaystyle\nu(\mathfrak{M})\thicksim\rho\langle v\rangle^{\gamma_{0}}\,,

where
\langle v\rangle=\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}
. Let
\mathcal{P}g
be the
L^{2}_{v}
projection with respect to
\mathcal{N}
. Then it is well-known (see for example
[
9
]
) that there exists a positive number
c_{0}>0
such that

(1.13)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}g,g\rangle\geq c_{0}\|(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})g\|^{2}_{\nu}

for
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
given in Proposition
1.1
.

For each
k\geq 1
,
\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}
can be decomposed as the macroscopic and microscopic parts:

\displaystyle\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Big\{\tfrac{\rho_{k}}{\rho}+u_{k}\cdot\tfrac{v-\mathfrak{u}}{T}+\tfrac{\theta_{k}}{6T}(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-3)\Big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})\,.

Following
[
21
]
, for
k\geq 1
,

(1.14)

(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})\big(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\big)=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\Big(-\tfrac{(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})F_{k-2}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}B(F_{i},F_{j})}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\Big)\,,

and the fluid variables
(\rho_{k},u_{k},\theta_{k})
obeys the following linear hyperbolic system

(1.15)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho_{k}+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho u_{k}+\rho_{k}\mathfrak{u})=0\,,\\
&\rho\big(\partial_{t}u_{k}+u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{k}\big)-\tfrac{\nabla_{x}(\rho T)}{\rho}\rho_{k}+\nabla_{x}\Big(\tfrac{\rho\theta_{k}+3T\rho_{k}}{3}\Big)=\mathcal{F}^{\bot}_{u}(F_{k})\,,\\
&\rho\Big(\partial_{t}\theta_{k}+\mathfrak{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\theta_{k}+\tfrac{2}{3}\big(\theta_{k}\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathfrak{u}+3T\mathrm{div}_{x}u_{k}\big)+3u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{x}T\Big)=\mathcal{G}^{\bot}_{\theta}(F_{k})\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
is the smooth solution of compressible Euler equations (
1.8
), the source terms
\mathcal{F}^{\bot}_{u}(F_{k})
and
\mathcal{G}^{\bot}_{\theta}(F_{k})
are defined as

(1.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\bot}_{u,i}(F_{k})=-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{x_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T\mathcal{A}_{ij}\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\mathrm{d}v\ (i=1,2,3)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\bot}_{\theta}(F_{k})=-\mathrm{div}_{x}\Big(2T^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathcal{B}\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\mathrm{d}v+\sum_{j=1}^{3}2T\mathfrak{u}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathcal{A}\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\mathrm{d}v\Big)-2\mathfrak{u}\cdot\mathcal{F}^{\bot}_{u}(F_{k})\,.

Here
\mathcal{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
and
\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
are the Burnett functions with entries

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\Big\{\tfrac{(v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i})(v_{j}-\mathfrak{u}_{j})}{T}-\delta_{ij}\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{3T}\Big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\quad(1\leq i,j\leq 3)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{2\sqrt{T}}\Big(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-5\Big)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\quad(1\leq i\leq 3)\,.

Finally, the initial data of (
1.15
) are imposed on

(1.18)

\displaystyle(\rho_{k},u_{k},\theta_{k})(0,x)=(\rho_{k}^{in},u_{k}^{in},\theta_{k}^{in})(x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\,,\quad k=1,2,3\,,\cdots.

1.3.2.
Viscous boundary layer expansion

In viscous layer, the scaled normal coordinate is needed:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\zeta=\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,.

As shown in Sone’s book
[
38
]
, the viscous boundary layer expansion has the form

(1.20)

\displaystyle F^{b}_{\varepsilon}(t,\bar{x},\zeta)\thicksim\sum_{k\geq 1}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}F^{b}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\zeta,v)\,,

where, throughout our paper, the far field condition is always assumed:

(1.21)

\displaystyle F^{b}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\zeta,v)\to 0\,,\quad\textrm{as }\zeta\to+\infty\,.

By similar calculation in
[
23
]
, plugging
F_{\varepsilon}+F^{b}_{\varepsilon}
into the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1}:\quad

\displaystyle 0=B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{b}_{1})+B(F^{b}_{1},\mathfrak{M}^{0})\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0}:\quad

\displaystyle v_{3}\cdot\partial_{\zeta}F^{b}_{1}=\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{b}_{2})+B(F^{b}_{2},\mathfrak{M}^{0})\big]+[B(F^{0}_{1},F^{b}_{1})+B(F^{b}_{1},F^{0}_{1})]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+B(F^{b}_{1},F^{b}_{1})+\zeta\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(1)},F^{b}_{1})+B(F^{b}_{1},\mathfrak{M}^{(1)})\big]\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}:\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{b}_{1}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}F^{b}_{1}+v_{3}\cdot\partial_{\zeta}F^{b}_{2}=\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{b}_{3})+B(F^{b}_{3},\mathfrak{M}^{0})\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\tfrac{\zeta}{1!}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(1)},F^{b}_{2})+B(F^{b}_{2},\mathfrak{M}^{(1)})\big]+\tfrac{\zeta^{2}}{2!}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(2)},F^{b}_{1})+B(F^{b}_{1},\mathfrak{M}^{(2)})\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\big[B(F^{0}_{1},F^{b}_{2})+B(F^{b}_{2},F^{0}_{1})\big]+\big[B(F^{0}_{2},F^{b}_{1})+B(F^{b}_{1},F^{0}_{2})\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\tfrac{\zeta}{1!}\big[B(F^{(1)}_{1},F^{b}_{1})+B(F^{b}_{1},F^{(1)}_{1})\big]+\big[B(F^{b}_{1},F^{b}_{2})+B(F^{b}_{2},F^{b}_{1})\big]\,,

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}:\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{b}_{k}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}F^{b}_{k}+v_{3}\cdot\partial_{\zeta}F^{b}_{k+1}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k+2\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}B(F^{b}_{i},F^{b}_{j})+\Big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{b}_{k+2})+B(F^{b}_{k+2},\mathfrak{M}^{0})\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l+j=k+2\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N\,,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}\Big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(l)},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},\mathfrak{M}^{(l)})\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k+2\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big[B(F^{0}_{i},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},F^{0}_{i})\Big]

(1.22)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+l=k+2\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N\,,i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}\Big[B(F^{(l)}_{i},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},F^{(l)}_{i})\Big]\quad{\text{for}}\ k\geq 1\,,

where the Taylor expansion at
x_{3}=0
is used:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{M}^{0}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq N}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}\mathfrak{M}^{(l)}+\tfrac{\zeta^{N+1}}{(N+1)!}\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{(N+1)}\,,

\displaystyle F_{i}=F_{i}^{0}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq N}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}F_{i}^{(l)}+\tfrac{\zeta^{N+1}}{(N+1)!}\widetilde{F}_{i}^{(N+1)}\,.

Here the number
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
will be chosen later.

Let

(1.23)

\displaystyle f^{b}_{k}=\tfrac{F^{b}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\,,

which can be decomposed as

\displaystyle f^{b}_{k}=\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k}+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k}=\left\{\tfrac{\rho^{b}_{k}}{\rho^{0}}+u^{b}_{k}\cdot\tfrac{v-\mathfrak{u}^{0}}{T^{0}}+\tfrac{\theta^{b}_{k}}{6T^{0}}(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}^{0}|^{2}}{T^{0}}-3)\right\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k}\,.

Here
u^{b}_{k}=(u^{b}_{k,1},u^{b}_{k,2},u^{b}_{k,3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Furthermore, let

(1.24)

\displaystyle p^{b}_{k}=\tfrac{\rho^{0}\theta^{b}_{k}+3T^{0}\rho^{b}_{k}}{3}\,.

Following the Theorem 1.1 of
[
23
]
, the following relations are obyed

(1.25)

\displaystyle u^{b}_{1,3}(t,\bar{x},\zeta)\equiv 0\,,\ p^{b}_{1}(t,\bar{x},\zeta)\equiv 0\,,\ \forall(t,\bar{x},\zeta)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\,,

and
(u^{b}_{k,1},u^{b}_{k,2},\theta^{b}_{k})

(k\geq 1)
satisfy the following
linear compressible Prandtl-type
equations
1
1

1

In
[
23
]
, the same system was named as a linear parabolic system.

(1.26)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \rho^{0}(\partial_{t}+\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}})u^{b}_{k,i}+\rho^{0}(\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{3}\zeta+u^{0}_{1,3})\partial_{\zeta}u^{b}_{k,i}+&\rho^{0}\bar{u}^{b}_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{i}+\tfrac{\partial_{x_{3}}p^{0}}{3T^{0}}\theta^{b}_{k}\\
=&\mu(T^{0})\partial_{\zeta}^{2}u^{b}_{k,i}+\mathsf{f}^{b}_{k-1,i}\ (i=1,2)\,,\\
\rho^{0}\partial_{t}\theta^{b}_{k}+\rho^{0}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\theta^{b}_{k}+\rho^{0}\big(\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}\zeta+\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{1,3}\big)\partial_{\zeta}\theta^{b}_{k}+&\tfrac{2}{3}\rho^{0}\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathfrak{u}^{0}\theta^{b}_{k}\\
=&\tfrac{3}{5}\kappa(T^{0})\partial_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{b}_{k}+\mathsf{g}^{b}_{k-1}\,,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to\infty}(\bar{u}^{b}_{k},\theta^{b}_{k})(t,\bar{x},\zeta)=&0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

and
(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k+1}
,
u^{b}_{k+1,3}
,
p^{b}_{k+1}
are determined by the equations

(1.27)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})

\displaystyle f^{b}_{k+1}=(\mathcal{L}^{0})^{-1}\Big\{-(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})(v_{3}\partial_{\zeta}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k})+\tfrac{\zeta}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(1)},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k})

\displaystyle+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k},\mathfrak{M}^{(1)})\big]+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(F^{0}_{1},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k},F^{0}_{1})\big]

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{1},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f^{b}_{k},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{1})\big]\Big\}+J^{b}_{k-1}\,,

and

(1.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{\zeta}u^{b}_{k+1,3}=-\frac{1}{\rho^{0}}(\partial_{t}\rho^{b}_{k}+\mathrm{div}_{\bar{x}}(\rho^{0}\bar{u}^{b}_{k}+\rho^{b}_{k}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}))\,,\ \lim_{\zeta\to\infty}u^{b}_{k+1,3}(t,\bar{x},\zeta)=0\,,

and

(1.29)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\zeta}p^{b}_{k+1}=-\rho^{0}\partial_{t}u^{b}_{k,3}-\rho^{0}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\bar{x}}u^{b}_{k,3}+\rho^{0}\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{3}u^{b}_{k,3}+\tfrac{4}{3}\mu(T^{0})\partial_{\zeta\zeta}u^{b}_{k,3}\\
-\tfrac{4}{3}\rho^{0}\partial_{\zeta}\Big[\big(\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}\zeta+u^{0}_{1,3}\big)u^{b}_{k,3}\Big]-\partial_{\zeta}\langle T^{0}\mathcal{A}^{0}_{33},J^{b}_{k-1}\rangle+W^{b}_{k-1,3}\,,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to\infty}\theta^{b}_{k+1}(t,\bar{x},\zeta)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where the source terms
\mathsf{f}^{b}_{k-1,i}\ (i=1,2)
and
\mathsf{g}^{b}_{k-1}
are

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}^{b}_{k-1,i}=-\rho^{0}\partial_{\zeta}[(\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{i}\zeta+u^{0}_{1,i}+u^{b}_{1,i})u^{b}_{k,3}]-(\partial_{x_{i}}-\tfrac{\partial_{x_{i}}p^{0}}{p^{0}})p^{b}_{k}

\displaystyle+W^{b}_{k-1,i}-T^{0}\partial_{\zeta}\langle J^{b}_{k-1},\mathcal{A}^{0}_{3i}\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}^{b}_{k-1}=-\rho^{0}\partial_{\zeta}\Big[\big(3\partial_{\zeta}T^{0}\zeta+\theta^{0}_{1}+\theta^{b}_{1}\big)u^{b}_{k,3}\Big]+\tfrac{3}{5}H^{b}_{k-1}-\tfrac{6}{5}(T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}}\partial_{\zeta}\langle J^{b}_{k-1},\mathcal{B}^{0}_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle+\tfrac{3}{5}\big\{2\partial_{t}+2\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}+\tfrac{10}{3}\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathfrak{u}^{0}\big\}p^{b}_{k}\,,

and

(1.31)

\displaystyle W^{b}_{k-1,i}=-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{x_{j}}\langle T^{0}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k},\mathcal{A}^{0}_{ij}\rangle\,,\qquad{\text{for}}\ i=1,2,3\,,

(1.32)

\displaystyle H^{b}_{k-1}=-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{x_{j}}\langle(T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{B}^{0}_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{2}2T^{0}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{l}\mathcal{A}^{0}_{jl},(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k}\rangle-2\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\bar{W}^{b}_{k-1}\,,

and

\displaystyle J^{b}_{k-1}=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{0})^{-1}\Big\{-(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})\Big(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big\{\partial_{t}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\big\}F^{b}_{k-1}\Big)-(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})\big(v_{3}\partial_{\zeta}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0}\big)f^{b}_{k}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l+j=k+1\,,\\
2\leq l\leq N,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(l)},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j},\mathfrak{M}^{(l)})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l+j=k+1\,,\\
i\geq 2,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(F^{0}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j},F^{0}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l+j=k+1\,,\\
i,j\geq 2\end{subarray}}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=k+1\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}\big[B(F^{(l)}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{j},F^{(l)}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\zeta}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(1)},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k},\mathfrak{M}^{(1)})\big]

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(F^{0}_{1},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k},F^{0}_{1})\big]

(1.33)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{1},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{b}_{1})\big]\Big\}\,.

We emphasize that
W^{b}_{k-1}
,
H^{b}_{k-1}
and
J^{b}_{k-1}
depend on
f^{b}_{j}

(1\leq j\leq k-1)
. Moreover, when
k=1
,
J^{b}_{0}=W^{b}_{0}=H^{b}_{0}=\mathsf{f}^{b}_{0}=\mathsf{g}^{b}_{0}=0
. Actually, by (
1.50
) below,
u_{1,3}^{0}=0
in (
1.26
). Finally, the initial conditions of (
1.26
) are imposed on

(1.34)

\displaystyle(\bar{u}^{b}_{k},\theta^{b}_{k})(0,\bar{x},\zeta)=(\bar{u}^{b,in}_{k},\theta^{b,in}_{k})(\bar{x},\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\,,\quad k=1,2,3\,,\cdots

with
\lim_{\zeta\to\infty}(\bar{u}^{b,in}_{k},\theta^{b,in}_{k})(\bar{x},\zeta)=0
.

1.3.3.
Knudsen boundary layer expansion

In Knudsen layer, the new scaled normal coordinate is introduced:

(1.35)

\displaystyle\xi=\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon}\,.

Then the Knudsen boundary expansion is defined as

(1.36)

\displaystyle F^{bb}_{\varepsilon}(t,\bar{x},\xi,v)\thicksim\sum_{k\geq 1}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}F^{bb}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\xi,v)\,.

From plugging
F_{\varepsilon}+F^{b}_{\varepsilon}+F^{bb}_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.1
),

(1.37)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1}:

\displaystyle v_{3}\cdot\partial_{\xi}F^{bb}_{1}=B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{bb}_{1})+B(F^{bb}_{1},\mathfrak{M}^{0})

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0}:

\displaystyle v_{3}\cdot\partial_{\xi}F^{bb}_{2}-\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{bb}_{2})+B(F^{bb}_{2},\mathfrak{M}^{0})\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B(F^{0}_{1}+F^{b,0}_{1},F^{bb}_{1})+B(F^{bb}_{1},F^{0}_{1}+F^{b,0}_{1})+B(F^{bb}_{1},F^{bb}_{1})

\displaystyle......

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}:

\displaystyle v_{3}\cdot\partial_{\xi}F^{bb}_{k+2}-\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{0},F^{bb}_{k+2})+B(F^{bb}_{k+2},\mathfrak{M}^{0})\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\big\{\partial_{t}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\big\}F^{bb}_{k}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+2l=k+2\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi^{l}}{l!}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(l)},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},\mathfrak{M}^{(l)})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k+2\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\big[B(F^{0}_{i}+F^{b,0}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},F^{0}_{i}+F^{b,0}_{i})+B(F^{bb}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+2l+j=k+2\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi^{l}}{l!}\big[B(F^{(l)}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},F^{(l)}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+l+j=k+2\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi^{l}}{l!}\big[B(F^{b,(l)}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},F^{b,(l)}_{i})\big]\,,

where the Taylor expansion of
F^{b}_{i}
at
\zeta=0
is utilized:

\displaystyle F_{i}^{b}=F_{i}^{b,0}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq N}\tfrac{\xi^{l}}{l!}F_{i}^{b,(l)}+\tfrac{\xi^{N+1}}{(N+1)!}\widetilde{F}_{i}^{b,(N+1)}\,.

Similar in viscous layer, let
f^{bb}_{k}=\tfrac{F^{bb}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}
, then (
1.37
) can be rewritten as

(1.38)

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{\xi}f^{bb}_{k}+\mathcal{L}^{0}f^{bb}_{k}=S^{bb}_{k}\,,\quad k\geq 1\,,

where
S^{bb}_{k}=S^{bb}_{k,1}+S^{bb}_{k,2}
with

\displaystyle S^{bb}_{k,1}=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{P}^{0}\Big\{\frac{(\partial_{t}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}})F^{bb}_{k-2}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\Big\}\in\mathcal{N}^{0}\,,

\displaystyle S^{bb}_{k,2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+2l=k\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi^{l}}{l!}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(l)},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j},\mathfrak{M}^{(l)})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(F^{0}_{i}+F^{b,0}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j},F^{0}_{i}+F^{b,0}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+2l+j=k\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi^{l}}{l!}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(F^{(l)}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j},F^{(l)}_{i})\big]

(1.39)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+l+j=k\,,\\
1\leq l\leq N,i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi^{l}}{l!}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\big[B(F^{b,(l)}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j})+B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j},F^{b,(l)}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\,,\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}B(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{i},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}f^{bb}_{j})-(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})\Big\{\frac{\{\partial_{t}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\}F^{bb}_{k-2}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\Big\}\in(\mathcal{N}^{0})^{\bot}\,.

Here the notation
F^{bb}_{-1}=F^{bb}_{0}=0
are used, and

\displaystyle S^{bb}_{1}=S^{bb}_{1,1}=S^{bb}_{1,2}=0\,,\ S^{bb}_{2,1}=\mathcal{P}^{0}S^{bb}_{2}=0\,.

Lemma 1.1

(
[
1
]
)
.

We assume that

(1.40)

\displaystyle S^{bb}_{k,1}=\big\{a_{k}+b_{k}\cdot(v-\mathfrak{u}^{0})+c_{k}|v-\mathfrak{u}^{0}|^{2}\big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}

satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\to\infty}e^{\eta\xi}|(a_{k},b_{k},c_{k})(t,\bar{x},\xi)|=0

for some positive constant
\eta>0
. Then there exists a function

f^{bb}_{k,1}=\big\{\Psi_{k}v_{3}+\Phi_{k,1}v_{3}(v_{1}-\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{1})+\Phi_{k,2}v_{3}(v_{2}-\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{2})+\Phi_{k,3}+\Theta_{k}v_{3}|v-\mathfrak{u}^{0}|^{2}\big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}

such that
v_{3}\partial_{\xi}f^{bb}_{k,1}-S^{bb}_{k,1}\in(\mathcal{N}^{0})^{\bot}
, where

(1.41)

\begin{array}[]{l}\Psi_{k}(t,\bar{x},\xi)=-\int_{\xi}^{+\infty}\big(\tfrac{2}{T^{0}}a_{k}+3c_{k}\big)(t,\bar{x},s)\mathrm{d}s\,,\\[4.2679pt]
\Phi_{k,i}(t,\bar{x},\xi)=-\int_{\xi}^{+\infty}\tfrac{1}{T^{0}}b_{k,i}(t,\bar{x},s)\mathrm{d}s\,,\ i=1,2\,,\\[4.2679pt]
\Phi_{k,3}(t,\bar{x},\xi)=-\int_{\xi}^{+\infty}b_{k,3}(t,\bar{x},s)\mathrm{d}s\,,\\[4.2679pt]
\Theta_{k}(t,\bar{x},\xi)=\tfrac{1}{5(T^{0})^{2}}\int_{\xi}^{+\infty}a_{k}(t,\bar{x},s)\mathrm{d}s\,.\end{array}

Moreover, there holds

\displaystyle|v_{3}\partial_{\xi}f^{bb}_{k,1}-S^{bb}_{k,1}|\leq C|(a_{k},b_{k},c_{k})(t,\bar{x},\xi)|\langle v\rangle^{4}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\,,

\displaystyle|f^{bb}_{k,1}(t,\bar{x},\xi,v)|\leq C\langle v\rangle^{3}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\int_{\xi}^{\infty}|(a_{k},b_{k},c_{k})|\to 0\textrm{ as }\xi\to\infty\,.

It is easy to know that
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
,
b_{1}=b_{2}=0
,
c_{1}=c_{2}=0
,
\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}=0\,,\ \Phi_{1,i}=\Phi_{2,i}=0\ (i=1,2,3)
and
\Theta_{1}=\Theta_{2}=0
. Denote by
f^{bb}_{k,2}=f^{bb}_{k}-f^{bb}_{k,1}
. We thereby see that

(1.42)

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{\xi}f^{bb}_{k,2}+\mathcal{L}^{0}f^{bb}_{k,2}=S^{bb}_{k,2}-(v_{3}\partial_{\xi}f^{bb}_{k,1}-S^{bb}_{k,1})\in(\mathcal{N}^{0})^{\bot}\,,\ \lim_{\xi\to\infty}f^{bb}_{k,2}(t,\bar{x},\xi,v)=0\,.

Once we impose the following boundary condition on (
1.42
):

(1.43)

\displaystyle f^{bb}_{k,2}(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})|_{v_{3}>0}=f^{bb}_{k,2}(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},-v_{3})+\mathbbm{f}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\bar{v},-v_{3})

for some function
\mathbbm{f}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\bar{v},v_{3})
only defined for
v_{3}<0
and extended to be 0 for
v_{3}>0
, Golse, Perthame and Sulem
[
15
]
proved that the solvability conditions of (
1.42
)-(
1.43
) were

(1.44)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\\
|v-\mathfrak{u}^{0}|^{2}\end{array}\right)v_{3}\mathbbm{f}_{k}(t,\bar{x},v)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}v\equiv 0\,.

1.3.4.
Expansions of the Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
1.2
)

In order to give suitable boundary conditions so that the interior expansions, viscous and Knudsen boundary layers are all well-posed, the expansions of
F_{\varepsilon}+F^{b}_{\varepsilon}+F^{bb}_{\varepsilon}
will be plugged into the Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
1.2
) and then utilize the solvability conditions (
1.44
) of the Knudsen boundary layer problem (
1.42
)-(
1.43
). More precisely, on
\Sigma_{-}
,

(1.45)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0}:

\displaystyle L^{R}\mathfrak{M}=0\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1}:

\displaystyle L^{R}(F_{1}+F_{1}^{b}+F_{1}^{bb})=L^{D}\mathfrak{M}\,,

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}:

\displaystyle L^{R}(F_{k}+F^{b}_{k}+F^{bb}_{k})=L^{D}(F_{k-1}+F^{b}_{k-1}+F^{bb}_{k-1})\ (k\geq 2)\,,

where the operators
L^{R}
and
L^{D}
are defined as

(1.46)

\displaystyle L^{R}F=(\gamma_{-}-L\gamma_{+})F\,,\quad L^{D}F=\sqrt{2\pi}(K\gamma_{+}-L\gamma_{+})F\,.

Actually,
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0}
-order of (
1.45
) can imply the slip boundary condition (
1.9
) of the compressible Euler system (
1.8
). Recalling the definitions

\displaystyle f_{k}=\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\,,\ f^{b}_{k}=\tfrac{F^{b}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\,,\ f^{bb}_{k}=\tfrac{F^{bb}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}}\,,\ f^{bb}_{k,2}=f^{bb}_{k}-f^{bb}_{k,1}

for
k\geq 1
, where the functions
f^{bb}_{k,1}
(
k\geq 1
) are given in Lemma
1.1
, we thereby obtain by direct calculation and (
1.45
) that the functions
\mathbbm{f}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\bar{v},v_{3})
(
k\geq 1
) in (
1.43
) are

(1.47)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{f}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\bar{v},v_{3})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\,,\qquad\textrm{if }v_{3}>0\,,\\[4.2679pt]
(f_{k}+f^{b}_{k}+f^{bb}_{k,1})(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})-(f_{k}+f^{b}_{k}+f^{bb}_{k,1})(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},-v_{3})\\
\qquad+\sqrt{2\pi}\big\{\big[\langle\gamma_{+}(f_{k-1}+f^{b}_{k-1}+f^{bb}_{k-1})\rangle_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\big]\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-(f_{k-1}+f^{b}_{k-1}+f^{bb}_{k-1})\big\}(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})\,,\ \textrm{if }v_{3}<0\,,\end{array}\right.

where the symbol
\langle\gamma_{+}f\rangle_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}
means

\displaystyle\langle\gamma_{+}f\rangle_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}=\sqrt{2\pi}\tfrac{M_{w}(v)}{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\int_{v\cdot n(x)>0}v\cdot n(x)(\gamma_{+}f)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}v\,,

and the notations
f_{0}=\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}
,
f^{b}_{0}=f^{bb}_{0}=0
are used, for simplicity of presentation.

Therefore, the following lemma holds:

Lemma 1.2
.

Let the local Maxwellian of the boundary
M_{w}=\mathfrak{M}^{0}
in (
1.2
) and
\mathbbm{f}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\bar{v},v_{3})
be given in (
1.47
). Then the solvability conditions (
1.44
) of the Knudsen boundary layer problem (
1.42
)-(
1.43
) imply that for
k\geq 1
, the linear hyperbolic system (
1.15
) has the following slip boundary condition

(1.48)

\displaystyle u_{k,3}(t,\bar{x},0)=-u^{b}_{k,3}(t,\bar{x},0)-T^{0}(\Psi_{k}+5T^{0}\Theta_{k})(t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{(\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+1)}{\rho^{0}}\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}v_{3}(f_{k-1}+f^{b}_{k-1}+f^{bb}_{k-1})(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\mathrm{d}v_{3}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tfrac{1}{\rho^{0}}\big[\partial_{t}\rho^{b}_{k-1}+\mathrm{div}_{\bar{x}}(\rho^{0}\bar{u}^{b}_{k-1}+\rho^{b}_{k-1}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0})\big](t,\bar{x},\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta-T^{0}(\Psi_{k}+5T^{0}\Theta_{k})(t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{(\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+1)}{\rho^{0}}\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}v_{3}(f_{k-1}+f^{b}_{k-1}+f^{bb}_{k-1})(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\mathrm{d}v_{3}\,,

and for
k\geq 2
, the linear Prandtl-type equations (
1.26
) are of the Robin-type boundary conditions

(1.49)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big(\partial_{\zeta}u^{b}_{k-1,i}-\tfrac{\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}(2+\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}})}{\mu(T^{0})}u^{b}_{k-1,i}\big)(t,\bar{x},0)=\Lambda^{b}_{k-1,i}(t,\bar{x}),i=1,2\,,\\[4.2679pt]
\big(\partial_{\zeta}\theta^{b}_{k-1}-\tfrac{\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}}{\kappa(T^{0})}(2\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{3}\rho^{0}+\tfrac{2}{3})\theta^{b}_{k-1}\big)(t,\bar{x},0)=\Lambda^{b}_{k-1,\theta}(t,\bar{x})\,,\end{array}\right.

where
\Psi_{k}
and
\Theta_{k}
are given in (
1.41
), and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k-1,i}^{b}(t,\bar{x})=\tfrac{\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}}{\mu(T^{0})}u_{k-1,i}(t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{\mu(T^{0})}\Big\{\rho^{0}(T^{0})^{2}\Phi_{k,i}+\rho^{0}\big[(u_{1,i}+u^{b}_{1,i})u^{b}_{k-1,3}\big]+T^{0}\langle\mathcal{A}^{0}_{3i},J^{b}_{k-2}+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f_{k}\rangle\Big\}(t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\mu(T^{0})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}(v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{i})v_{3}\big[(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})(f_{k-1}+f^{b}_{k-1})+f^{bb}_{k-1}\big](t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\mathrm{d}v_{3}\,,

and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k-1,\theta}^{b}

\displaystyle(t,\bar{x})=\tfrac{2(T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\kappa(T^{0})}\langle\mathcal{B}^{0}_{3},(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f_{k}+J^{b}_{k-2}\rangle(t,\bar{x},0)+\tfrac{5}{3\kappa(T^{0})}\rho^{0}\big[(\theta_{1}+\theta^{b}_{1})u^{b}_{k-1,3}\big](t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\rho^{0}T^{0}}{\kappa(T^{0})}\big\{10(T^{0})^{2}\Theta_{k}-(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{3}{2}T^{0}-2\rho^{0}(T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}})(\Psi_{k-1}+5T^{0}\Theta_{k-1})\big\}(t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}}{\kappa(T^{0})}\big\{(\tfrac{2}{3}\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2}\rho^{0}+2)\theta_{k-1}-4(\sqrt{T^{0}}-\tfrac{1}{\rho^{0}})(T^{0}\rho_{k-1}+p^{b}_{k-1})\big\}(t,\bar{x},0)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\kappa(T^{0})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}v_{3}(|v-\mathfrak{u}^{0}|^{2}-4\rho^{0}(T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}})

\displaystyle\times\big[(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})(f_{k-1}+f^{b}_{k-1})+f^{bb}_{k-1}\big](t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\mathrm{d}v_{3}

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\kappa(T^{0})}(\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+1)(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{3}{2}T^{0}-2\rho^{0}(T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}})

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}v_{3}(f_{k-2}+f^{b}_{k-2}+f^{bb}_{k-2})(t,\bar{x},0,\bar{v},v_{3})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\mathrm{d}v_{3}\,.

In particular, if
k=1
in (
1.48
), we have

(1.50)

\displaystyle u^{0}_{1,3}=u_{1,3}(t,\bar{x},0)=\sqrt{T^{0}}(\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+1)\,.

The proof of the lemma will be given in Section
4
. We remark that the Robin-type boundary condition (
1.49
) will be Neumann-type boundary values and
u_{1,3}^{0}
will vanish, if the Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
1.2
) is replaced by the specular reflection boundary condition, i.e., by letting
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=0
, see
[
23
]
.

1.3.5.
Truncations of the Hilbert expansion

Our goal is to prove the compressible Euler limit from the scaled Boltzmann equation by above Hilbert type expansion. The key point is to prove that the remainders of expansion will go to zero as the Knudsen number
\varepsilon\to 0
. Mathematically, this means we search for a special class of solutions of the original scaled Boltzmann equation for sufficiently small Knudsen number
\varepsilon
. Since the more terms are expanded, the more special the solutions are. We hope that the terms in the expansion are as less as possible.

Our truncated Hilbert expansion takes the following form:

(1.51)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\mathfrak{M}(t,x,v)+\sum_{k=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}\big\{F_{k}(t,x,v)+F_{k}^{b}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)+F_{k}^{bb}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon},v)\big\}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}F_{R,\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\geq 0\,.

Here
\mathfrak{M}
is governed by the compressible Euler system (
1.11
)-(
1.9
)-(
1.10
).

First, the interior expansions
F_{1}=\mathcal{P}(\tfrac{F_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}
and
F_{k}=\big\{\mathcal{P}(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})\big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}
(
k=2,3
), where
(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})
are given by (
1.14
), and the fluid variables
(\rho_{k},u_{k},\theta_{k})

(1\leq k\leq 3)
associated with
\mathcal{P}(\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})
are solutions of the linear hyperbolic system (
1.15
) with initial data (
1.18
) and slip boundary condition (
1.48
). In this sense,
F_{k}
(
1\leq k\leq 3
) are completely specified. For
k=4,5
, we take
F_{k}
such that their kernel (fluid) parts vanish, i.e.,
F_{k}=[(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})\tfrac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}]\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}
, which are defined by (
1.14
). They thereby are not completely known terms.

Second, the viscous boundary layer expansions
F_{1}^{b}=(\mathcal{P}^{0}f_{1}^{b})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}
and
F_{k}^{b}=\big\{\mathcal{P}^{0}f_{k}^{b}+(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f_{k}^{b}\big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}
(
k=2,3
), where the kinetic part
(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f_{k}^{b}
(
k=2,3
) are given by (
1.27
), and
\mathcal{P}^{0}f_{k}^{b}
(
1\leq k\leq 3
) correspond to the fluid variables
(\rho^{b}_{k},u^{b}_{k},\theta^{b}_{k})
.
(\bar{u}^{b}_{k},\theta^{b}_{k})
are solutions of the linear Prandtl-type system (
1.26
) with initial data (
1.34
) and Robin-type boundary conditions (
1.49
). The functions
(\rho^{b}_{k},u^{b}_{k,3})

(1\leq k\leq 3)
are expressed through equations (
1.24
), (
1.25
), (
1.28
) and (
1.29
), where the subscript
k+1
is replaced by
k
. Thus
F_{k}
(
1\leq k\leq 3
) are all known terms, so are
F^{b}_{k}
(
1\leq k\leq 3
). For
k=4,5
, we choose
F^{b}_{k}
such that their fluid variables vanish, hence,
F^{b}_{k}=[(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f^{b}_{k}]\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{0}}
, which are expressed by (
1.27
), which mean that they are not completely specified.

Finally, we observe from (
1.38
)-(
1.3.3
) and the boundary values (
1.43
)-(
1.47
) that the Knudsen boundary layer expansions
F^{bb}_{k}
depends on
F^{bb}_{i}
(
i\leq k-1
) and
F^{b}_{j}
,
F_{j}
(
j\leq k
). Here
F^{b}_{i}=F^{bb}_{i}=F_{i-1}=0
for subscript
i\leq 0
and
F_{0}=\mathfrak{M}
. Since
\mathfrak{M}
,
F_{k}
and
F^{b}_{k}
(
k=1,2,3
) are already known, we easily see that
F^{bb}_{k}
(
k=1,2,3
) are totally solved. Similarly,
F^{bb}_{4}
and
F^{bb}_{5}
are not completely solved. Consequently, the corresponding remainder shall be
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}F_{R,\varepsilon}
as in (
1.51
). We also emphasize that the number
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
appeared in the Taylor expansions before will be chosen by
N=4
for the Hilbert expansion (
1.51
).

Consequently, from plugging (
1.51
) into (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), we obtain the remainder equation

(1.52)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{R,\varepsilon}+

\displaystyle v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{R,\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}[B(\mathfrak{M},F_{R,\varepsilon})+B(F_{R,\varepsilon},\mathfrak{M})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{2}B(F_{R,\varepsilon},F_{R,\varepsilon})+R_{\varepsilon}+R^{b}_{\varepsilon}+R^{bb}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i-2}[B(F_{i}+F^{b}_{i}+F^{bb}_{i},F_{R,\varepsilon})+B(F_{R,\varepsilon},F_{i}+F^{b}_{i}+F^{bb}_{i})]

with Maxwell reflection type boundary condition

(1.53)

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}F_{R,\varepsilon}=(1-\alpha_{\varepsilon})L\gamma_{+}F_{R,\varepsilon}+\alpha_{\varepsilon}K\gamma_{+}F_{R,\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{2}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\quad\textrm{ on }\Sigma_{-}\,,

where

(1.54)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varepsilon}=\tfrac{\alpha_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}(K\gamma_{+}-L\gamma_{+})(F_{5}+F^{b}_{5}+F^{bb}_{5})\,,

and

(1.55)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}=-(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})(F_{4}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}F_{5})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 6\\
1\leq i,j\leq 5\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+j-6}B(F_{i},F_{j})\,,

and

(1.56)

\displaystyle R^{b}_{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle-(\partial_{t}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}})(F^{b}_{4}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}F^{b}_{5})-v_{3}\partial_{\zeta}F^{b}_{5}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+l\geq 6\\
1\leq j\leq 5,1\leq l\leq 4\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{j+l-6}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(l)},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},\mathfrak{M}^{(l)})]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 6\\
1\leq i,j\leq 5\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+j-6}[B(F^{0}_{i},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},F^{0}_{i})+B(F^{b}_{i},F^{b}_{j})]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+l\geq 6\\
1\leq i,j\leq 5,1\leq l\leq 4\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+j+l-6}\tfrac{\zeta^{l}}{l!}[B(F_{i}^{(l)},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},F_{i}^{(l)})]

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\zeta^{5}}{5!}\sum_{j=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{j-1}[B(\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{(5)}+\sum_{i=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i}\widetilde{F}^{(5)}_{i},F^{b}_{j})+B(F^{b}_{j},\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{(5)}+\sum_{i=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i}\widetilde{F}^{(5)}_{i})]\,,

and

\displaystyle R^{bb}_{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle-(\partial_{t}+\bar{v}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}})(F^{bb}_{4}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}F^{bb}_{5})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+2l\geq 6\\
1\leq j\leq 5,1\leq l\leq 4\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{j+2l-6}\tfrac{\xi^{l}}{l!}[B(\mathfrak{M}^{(l)},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},\mathfrak{M}^{(l)})]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 6\\
1\leq i,j\leq 5\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+j-6}[B(F^{0}_{i}+F^{b,0}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},F^{0}_{i}+F^{b,0}_{i})+B(F^{bb}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+2l\geq 6\\
1\leq i,j\leq 5,1\leq l\leq 4\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+j+2l-6}\tfrac{\xi^{l}}{l!}[B(\widetilde{F}^{(l)}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},\widetilde{F}^{(l)}_{i})]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+l\geq 6\\
1\leq i,j\leq 5,1\leq l\leq 4\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+j+l-6}\tfrac{\xi^{l}}{l!}[B(\widetilde{F}^{b,(l)}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})+B(F^{bb}_{j},\widetilde{F}^{b,(l)}_{i})]

\displaystyle+\tfrac{\xi^{5}}{5!}\sum_{j=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{j-1}[B(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{5}\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{(5)}+\sum_{i=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+5}\widetilde{F}^{(5)}_{i}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i}\widetilde{F}^{b,(5)}_{i},F^{bb}_{j})

(1.57)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+B(F^{bb}_{j},\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{5}\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}^{(5)}+\sum_{i=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i+5}\widetilde{F}^{(5)}_{i}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{i}\widetilde{F}^{b,(5)}_{i})]\,.

Furthermore, the following initial data are imposed on the remainder equation (
1.52
):

(1.58)

\displaystyle F_{R,\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}(x,v)\,,

which satisfies the compatibility condition on
\Sigma_{-}

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}=(1-\alpha_{\varepsilon})L\gamma_{+}F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}+\alpha_{\varepsilon}K\gamma_{+}F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{2}\Gamma_{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\,.

For the remainder
F_{R,\varepsilon}
, let

(1.59)

\displaystyle f_{R,\varepsilon}=\tfrac{F_{R,\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\,,\quad h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}=\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\tfrac{F_{R,\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}_{M}}}

for
\ell\geq 9-2\gamma_{0}
, where the global Maxwellian
\mathfrak{M}_{M}=\mathfrak{M}_{M}(v)
is introduced by
[
9
]

(1.60)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{M}=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi T_{M})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\Big\{-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2T_{M}}\Big\}\,.

Here the constant
T_{M}
satisfies

(1.61)

\displaystyle T_{M}<\max_{t\in[0,\tau],x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}T(t,x)<2T_{M}\,.

Then there exists constants
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
such that for some
\frac{1}{2}<z<1
and for each
(t,x,v)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the following inequality holds:

(1.62)

\displaystyle C_{1}\mathfrak{M}_{M}\leq\mathfrak{M}\leq C_{2}(\mathfrak{M}_{M})^{z}\,.

1.4.
Main results

We first introduce some notations for convenience for stating our main theorem. For multi-indexes
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{m}),\alpha^{\prime}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\alpha_{m}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
, the symbol
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
means
\alpha_{i}\leq\alpha_{i}^{\prime}(i=1,2,\cdots,m)
and
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}
.

In order to quantitatively describe the linear hyperbolic system (
1.15
) in the half-space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
, let

\partial^{\alpha}_{t,\bar{x}}=\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\,,

where
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, and let

(1.63)

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}=\sum_{|\alpha|+i\leq k}\|\partial_{t,\bar{x}}^{\alpha}\partial^{i}_{x_{3}}f(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}\,,\ \|g(t)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\partial_{t,\bar{x}}^{\alpha}g(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

for functions
f(t)=f(t,\bar{x},x_{3})
and
g(t)=g(t,\bar{x})
. We remark that, for a function
f=f(\bar{x},x_{3})
independent of the variable
t
,
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}
is equivalent to the standard Sobolev norm
\|f\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}
.

While characterizing quantitatively the linear Prandtl-type system (
1.26
) associated with the macroscopic parts of the viscous boundary layer, some new norms are required to be introduced. For
l\geq 0
, the weighted norm is defined as

(1.64)

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(1+\zeta)^{l}|f(\bar{x},\zeta)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\mathrm{d}\zeta\,.

We further introduce a weighted Sobolev space
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
for any
r,l\geq 0
. Denote by the 2D multi-index
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. For any
l,r\geq 0
, let

(1.65)

\displaystyle l_{j}=l+2(r-j)\,,\ 0\leq j\leq r\,.

We then introduce the norms

(1.66)

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{l,r,n}=\sum_{2\gamma+|\beta|=r-n}\|\partial_{t}^{\gamma}\partial_{\bar{x}}^{\beta}\partial_{\zeta}^{n}f(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l_{r}}}\ (0\leq n\leq r)\,,

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{l,r}=\sum_{n=0}^{r}\|f(t)\|^{2}_{l,r,n}=\sum_{2\gamma+|\beta|+n=r}\|\partial_{t}^{\gamma}\partial_{\bar{x}}^{\beta}\partial_{\zeta}^{n}f(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l_{r}}}\,,

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{r}_{l,n}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\|f(t)\|^{2}_{l,j,n}\ (n=0,1,\cdots,r)\,,

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{r}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\|f(t)\|^{2}_{l,j}=\sum_{n=0}^{r}\|f(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{r}_{l,n}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}

for function
f=f(t,\bar{x},\zeta)
. For
g=g(\bar{x},\zeta)
, let

(1.67)

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{r}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\sum_{|\beta|+n=j}\|\partial_{\bar{x}}^{\beta}\partial_{\zeta}^{n}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l_{j}}}\,.

Similarly, for function
h=h(t,\bar{x})
, let

(1.68)

\displaystyle\|h(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\|h(t)\|^{2}_{\Gamma,j}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\sum_{2\gamma+|\beta|=j}\|\partial_{t}^{\gamma}\partial_{\bar{x}}^{\beta}h(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,.

In the above norms, one order time derivative is equivalent to two orders space derivative.

We now clarify the initial data of the scaled Boltzmann equation (
1.1
). Let

(1.69)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{in}(x,v)=\tfrac{\rho^{in}(x)}{[2\pi T^{in}(x)]^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\Big\{-\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}^{in}(x)|^{2}}{2T^{in}(x)}\Big\}\,.

For
1\leq k\leq 5
,
F_{k}^{in}(x,v)
,
F_{k}^{b,in}(\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)
and
F^{bb,in}_{k}(\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon},v)
can be constructed by the same ways of constructing the expansions
F_{k}(t,x,v)
,
F^{b}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)
and
F^{bb}_{k}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon},v)
in Subsection
1.3.5
, respectively. More precisely, for
k=1,2,3
, it just replaces
\mathfrak{M}
,
(\rho_{k},u_{k},\theta_{k})
and
(\bar{u}^{b}_{k},\theta^{b}_{k})
by
\mathfrak{M}^{in}
,
(\rho_{k}^{in},u_{k}^{in},\theta_{k}^{in})
and
(\bar{u}^{b,in}_{k},\theta^{b,in}_{k})
, respectively. Here we further assume the initial data
(\rho_{k}^{in},u_{k}^{in},\theta_{k}^{in})
and
(\bar{u}^{b,in}_{k},\theta^{b,in}_{k})
(
1\leq k\leq 3
) compatibly satisfy the conditions (
1.48
) and (
1.49
), respectively. We impose the well-prepared initial data on the scaled Boltzmann equation (
1.1
)

(1.70)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=\mathfrak{M}^{in}(x,v)+\sum_{k=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}\big\{F_{k}^{in}(x,v)+F_{k}^{b,in}(\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)+F^{bb,in}_{k}(\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon},v)\big\}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}(x,v)\geq 0\,.

Furthermore, for the local Maxwellian distribution
M_{w}(t,x)
of the boundary given in (
1.4
), we take the special form

(1.71)

\displaystyle M_{w}(t,\bar{x},v)=\mathfrak{M}^{0}(t,\bar{x},v)\,.

We now state our main theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider the hard potential interaction
(0\leq\gamma_{0}\leq 1)
Boltzmann collision kernel
B
with an angular cutoff
(
see (
1.3
)
)
. Let
\ell\geq 9-2\gamma_{0}
, and integers
s_{0}
,
s_{k}
,
s_{k}^{b}
,
s_{k}^{bb}
,
l^{b}_{k}

(1\leq k\leq 3)
be described as in Proposition
2.1
. Assume that
\|(\rho^{in},\mathfrak{u}^{in},T^{in})\|_{H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}<\infty
and

(1.72)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{in}:=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\Big\{\|(\rho_{k}^{in},u_{k}^{in},\theta_{k}^{in})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}+\|(\bar{u}^{b,in}_{k},\theta_{k}^{b,in})\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s_{k}^{b}}_{l^{b}_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}\Big\}<\infty\,.

Let
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
be the solution to the compressible Euler equations (
1.8
) over the time interval
t\in[0,\tau]
constructed in Proposition
1.1
, which determines the local Maxwellian
\mathfrak{M}
defined in (
1.7
). For
1\leq k\leq 5
, let
F_{k}(t,x,v)
,
F_{k}^{b}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)
and
F_{k}^{bb}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon},v)
be constructed in Proposition
2.1
. The local Maxwellian
M_{w}(t,\bar{x},v)
of the boundary is assumed as in (
1.71
). There is a small constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{R}^{in}:=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})}\{\|\tfrac{F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}^{in}}}\|_{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}\|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\tfrac{F_{R,\varepsilon}^{in}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}_{M}}}\|_{\infty}\}<\infty\,,

then the scaled Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) with Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
1.2
) and well-prepared initial data (
1.70
) admits a unique solution for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
over the time interval
t\in[0,\tau]
with the expanded form (
1.51
), i.e.,

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\mathfrak{M}(t,x,v)+\sum_{k=1}^{5}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{k}\big\{F_{k}(t,x,v)+F_{k}^{b}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)+F_{k}^{bb}(t,\bar{x},\tfrac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon},v)\big\}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}F_{R,\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\geq 0\,,

where the remainder
F_{R,\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\Big\{\|\tfrac{F_{R,\varepsilon}(t)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\|_{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}

\displaystyle\|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\tfrac{F_{R,\varepsilon}(t)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}_{M}}}\|_{\infty}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C(\tau,\|(\rho^{in}-\rho_{\#},\mathfrak{u}^{in},T^{in}-T_{\#})\|_{H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})},\mathcal{E}^{in},\mathcal{E}_{R}^{in})<\infty\,.

Remark 1.1
.

Together with Proposition
2.1
, Theorem
1.1
shows that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\Big\{\|(\tfrac{F_{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{M}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})(t)\|_{2}+\|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}(\tfrac{F_{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{M}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}_{M}}})(t)\|_{\infty}\Big\}\leq C\sqrt{\varepsilon}\to 0\,.

Therefore, while imposed on the well-prepared initial data and accommodation coefficients
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we have justified the hydrodynamic limit from the Boltzmann equation with Maxwell reflection boundary condition to the compressible Euler system with slip boundary condition for the half-space problem.

1.5.
Sketch of ideas and novelties

Contrast with the work
[
23
]
, where the specular reflection boundary condition (in short, SRBC) for the scaled Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) was considered, we consider the Maxwell reflection boundary condition (briefly, MRBC). We first point out the formal differences between
[
23
]
and our work. For the MRBC case, the slip boundary value
u_{1,3}(t,\bar{x},0)=\sqrt{T^{0}}(\rho^{0}\sqrt{T^{0}}+1)>0
(see (
1.50
)) for the linear hyperbolic system
(\rho_{1},u_{1},\theta_{1})
(see (
2.2
)) does not vanish, and for the SRBC case,
u_{1,3}(t,\bar{x},0)\equiv 0
. The types of boundary conditions for the linear compressible Prandtl-type equations (
1.26
) are also different. For the MRBC case, we obtain Robin-type boundary conditions (see in (
1.49
)), while the Neumann-type was derived in the SRBC case. We emphasize that the structures of boundary energy will be more subtle in the Robin-type than that in the Neumann-type while proving the existence of the system (
1.26
). Furthermore, the way to deal with the boundary integral
-\tfrac{1}{2}\iint_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}|f_{R,\varepsilon}(t,\bar{x},0,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\bar{x}
in
L^{2}
estimates on the remainder
f_{R,\varepsilon}
is even much more different. For the SRBC case, the boundary integral is zero. However, for the MRBC case, a key boundary energy dissipative rate
\iint_{\Sigma_{-}}|v\cdot n(x)||L\gamma_{+}f_{R,\varepsilon}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\Sigma_{-}}
is implied by the boundary integral, provided that the wall Maxwellian
M_{w}(t,\bar{x},v)
in (
1.4
) is assumed to be
\mathfrak{M}^{0}
. For details see Lemma
3.1
. There are also many other small and not so essential differences, we will not list them all here.

In the Hilbert expansion approach, we hope the number of expanded terms as small as possible. In this paper, we want to employ
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
framework (see
[
20
,
22
]
, for instance) to prove that the remainder
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}F_{R,\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
is a higher order infinitesimal as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We claim that the expansion (
1.51
) is
optimal
in this framework. The uniform
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
estimates rely on an interplay between
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
estimates for the Boltzmann equation. On one hand, following
[
22
]
,
L^{2}
-estimates will be such that the norm
\|f_{R,\varepsilon}\|_{2}
can be bounded by
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}\|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}\|_{\infty}
. On the other hand, the norm
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}\|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}\|_{\infty}
will be dominated by
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}\|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(0)\|_{\infty}+\|f_{R,\varepsilon}\|_{2}
. For details see Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
below. Based on the previous interplay, we can obtain the uniform bound of the quantity

(1.73)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\Big\{\|f_{R,\varepsilon}(t)\|_{2}+\|\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(t)\|_{\infty}\Big\}\leq C\,,

provided that
\|f_{R,\varepsilon}(0)\|_{2}+\|\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(0)\|_{\infty}\leq C
initially holds uniformly for small
\varepsilon>0
. Consequently, we see that
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{4}F_{R,\varepsilon}
is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
in
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
framework.

Moreover, the order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}
occurred in the uniform bound (
1.73
) is independent of the number of expanded terms. Indeed, no matter how many terms are expanded, the remainder equations will contain a common linear structure

(1.74)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{R,\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{R,\varepsilon}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{L}f_{R,\varepsilon}=\textrm{ some other terms}\,.

In
L^{2}
estimates, the hypocoercivity of the operator
\mathcal{L}
in (
1.13
) produces a dissipative rate
\tfrac{c_{0}}{\varepsilon}\|(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P})f_{R,\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{\nu}
, which is the key point to deal with the singular terms. However, in
L^{\infty}
estimates, after transforming
f_{R,\varepsilon}
to
h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}
(by using (
1.59
)), the equation (
1.74
) will be integrated along the trajectory
[X_{cl}(s;t,x,v),V_{cl}(s;t,x,v)]
(see (
3.2
) below). We then have the pointwise estimate

\displaystyle|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(t,x,v)|\leq

\displaystyle C\|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(0)\|_{\infty}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{exp}\Big\{-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{s}^{t}\nu(\phi)\mathrm{d}\phi\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big|\Bbbk_{\ell}\big(V_{cl}(s),v^{\prime}\big)\Bbbk_{\ell}(v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\big|\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{exp}\Big\{-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{s_{1}}^{s}\nu(v^{\prime})(\phi)\mathrm{d}\phi\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big|h^{\ell}_{R,\varepsilon}\big(s_{1},X_{cl}(s_{1};s,X_{cl}(s),v^{\prime}),v^{\prime\prime}\big)\big|\mathrm{d}v^{\prime\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\textrm{ some other controllable quantities}\,,

where
\Bbbk_{\ell}(v,v^{\prime})
satisfies (
3.21
). As shown in Case 3b of the proof for Lemma
3.2
, the second term in RHS of the previous inequality will be bounded by

\displaystyle\tfrac{C}{N_{0}}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(s)\|_{\infty}+\tfrac{C}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|f_{R,\varepsilon}(s)\|_{2}

for
N_{0}>0
large enough. Comparing to the
L^{2}
estimates, we fail to seek a similar hypocoercivity (
1.13
) in
L^{\infty}
estimates, so that the singular term
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{L}f_{R,\varepsilon}
is roughly treated as a source term. Therefore, there is a
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{3}
-order disparity between
\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|h_{R,\varepsilon}^{\ell}(s)\|_{\infty}
and
\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|f_{R,\varepsilon}(s)\|_{2}
. Once we expand less number of the terms than that in (
1.51
), the corresponding remainder
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m}F_{R,\varepsilon}
(
m\leq 3
) will only be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-3})
, which is failed to achieve our goal. Nevertheless, this disparity maybe not intrinsic, and perhaps be covered in some other frameworks. So, we may expand less terms in the Hilbert expansion when proving the compressible Euler limit by employing the Hilbert expansion approach.

When constructing the viscous boundary layers
F^{b}_{k}
, one of key points is to focus on the existence of the smooth solutions to the linear compressible Prandtl-type equations (
2.6
) (or (
1.26
)) with Robin-type boundary conditions (
2.8
) (or (
1.49
)), which derives from the Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
1.2
). Note that the linear compressible Prandtl-type system (
2.6
) is a degenerated parabolic system with nontrivial Robin-type boundary values (
2.8
). For the linear system, we can introduce some explicit functions to zeroize the inhomogeneous boundary values (see (
5.4
)), which actually converts the boundary values into the form of external force terms
(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})
, see (
5.6
) below. We therefore obtain the system (
5.5
) with zero boundary values. Due to (
5.5
) is a degenerated parabolic system, we can construct a linear parabolic approximate system (
5.8
) while proving the existence of (
5.5
). When deriving the uniform bounds of the approximate system in the space
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}))
, the
key point
is to deal with the boundary values of the higher order normal
\zeta
-derivatives on
\{\zeta=0\}
. The idea is employing the structures of equations to convert the higher normal
\zeta
-derivatives to the values of tangential
\bar{x}
-derivatives and time derivatives on
\{\zeta=0\}
, see Lemma
5.2
below. Then we can find a key boundary energy

\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\|B_{c}(u,\theta)(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\int_{0}^{\tau}\|B_{c}(u,\theta)(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mathrm{d}s\,,

where
B_{c}(u,\theta)=(u,\theta)|_{\zeta=0}-(\tfrac{1}{R_{u}}b,\tfrac{1}{R_{\theta}}a)
(see Lemma
2.2
).

Furthermore, the solution
(u,\theta)(t,\bar{x},\zeta)
to the linear compressible Prandtl-type equations (
2.6
) will produce a loss of derivatives with respect to the boundary values and source terms. More precisely, as shown in Lemma
2.2
, if
(u,\theta)(t,\bar{x},\zeta)
is in
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}))
, the source terms
(f,g)(t,\bar{x},\zeta)
and the boundary values
(b,a)(t,\bar{x})
should be in
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k+1}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}))
and
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k+3}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, respectively. The reasons are as follows. For the system (
5.5
) zeroed boundary values, we shall use the structures of the equations to dominate the boundary values of higher order
\zeta
-derivatives, which involve the new source term
(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})
restricted on
\{\zeta=0\}
. As shown in Lemma
5.3
, in order to control the boundary values in the space
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, the
(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})|_{\zeta=0}
should be also in
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Combining the trace inequalities in Lemma
5.1
, the new source terms should be in
\mathbb{H}^{k+1}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
, so should be the source terms
(f,g)
. Noticing that
\partial_{t}(u_{b},\theta_{a})\thicksim\partial_{t}(b,a)
occur in the new source terms
(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})
and a first order time derivative is equivalent to a second order tangential
\bar{x}
-derivative in the space
\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we therefore see that the boundary values
(b,a)(t,\bar{x})
should be in
L^{\infty}(0,\tau;\mathbb{H}^{k+3}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

1.6.
Organization of this paper

In next section, the uniform bounds to the expansions
F_{k}
,
F_{k}^{b}
and
F_{k}^{bb}
(
1\leq k\leq 5
) appeared in (
1.51
) is derived. Section
3
devotes to prove Theorem
1.1
by using the
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
arguments. More precisely, the
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
estimates are given. In Section
4
, it is formally derived the boundary conditions for the linear hyperbolic system (
2.2
) and linear compressible Prandtl-type equations (
1.26
), namely, prove Lemma
1.2
. In Section
5
, the proof for the existence of the linear compressible Prandtl-type system (
2.6
) with Robin-type boundary values (
2.8
) is given. Hence, we prove Lemma
2.2
.

2.
Uniform bounds to the solutions of expansions

In this section, we aim at deriving the uniform bounds to the expansions
F_{k}
,
F_{k}^{b}
and
F_{k}^{bb}
(
1\leq k\leq 5
).

2.1.
Some auxiliary systems

In the expansions (
1.51
), there are some common types systems, which are only distinguished by some known coefficients. For
1\leq k\leq 3
, the fluid parts of
F_{k}
satisfy a linear hyperbolic system, and that of
F_{k}^{b}
obey a linear compressible Prandtl-type systems with Robin-type boundary conditions. Moreover,
F_{k}^{bb}
(
1\leq k\leq 5
) subject to the well-known Knudsen boundary layer equations. We summarize quantitatively these system as follows.

We alway assume that
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
over the time interval
t\in[0,\tau]
is the smooth solution to the compressible Euler system (
1.1
) given in Proposition
1.1
and
p=\rho T
. We also employ the notation

(2.1)

\displaystyle E_{k}:=\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\|(\rho-\rho_{\#},\mathfrak{u},T-T_{\#})(t)\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}

for any integer
k\geq 3
.

Let
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u},\tilde{\theta})(t,x)
satisfy the following linear hyperbolic system:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\tilde{\rho}+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho\tilde{u}+\tilde{\rho}\mathfrak{u})=0\,,\\[4.2679pt]
\rho(\partial_{t}\tilde{u}+\tilde{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\tilde{u})-\frac{\nabla_{x}p}{\rho}\tilde{\rho}+\nabla\big(\tfrac{\rho\tilde{\theta}+3T\tilde{\rho}}{3}\big)=f\,,\\[4.2679pt]
\rho\big[\partial_{t}\tilde{\theta}+\tfrac{2}{3}\big(\tilde{\theta}\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathfrak{u}+3T\mathrm{div}_{x}\tilde{u}\big)+\mathfrak{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\tilde{\theta}+3\tilde{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}T\big]=g\,,\end{array}\right.

with
(t,x)\in(0,\tau)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. We impose the boundary condition

(2.3)

\tilde{u}_{3}(t,\bar{x},0)=d(t,x)\,,\forall(t,\bar{x})\in(0,\tau)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

and initial condition

(2.4)

(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u},\tilde{\theta})(0,x)=(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{u}_{0},\tilde{\theta}_{0})(x)\,.

Recalling the definitions of the space
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
and
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in (
1.63
), we then have the following lemma for the local existence of smooth solution for the linear hyperbolic system (
2.2
).

Lemma 2.1

(
[
23
]
)
.

Assume that

\mathbb{E}_{0}:=\|(\tilde{\rho}^{0},\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{\theta}^{0})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}+\sup_{t\in(0,\tau)}\big[\|(f,g)(t)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}+\|d(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k+2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\big]<+\infty

with
k\geq 3
, and the compatibility condition is satisfied for the initial data. Then there exists a unique smooth solution to (
2.2
)-(
2.4
) with boundary condition (
2.3
) for
t\in[0,\tau]
, such that

(2.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}{\|(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u},\tilde{\theta})(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})}^{2}}\leq C(\tau,E_{k+2})\mathbb{E}_{0}\,.

As shown in (
1.26
) and Lemma
1.2
, to construct the solution of viscous boundary layer, the following linear compressible Prandtl type system of
(u,\theta)=(u_{1},u_{2},\theta)(t,\bar{x},\zeta)
should be considered: for
i=1,2
,

(2.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho^{0}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\rho^{0}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}u_{i}+\rho^{0}(\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{3}\zeta+u^{0}_{1,3})\partial_{\zeta}u_{i}+\rho^{0}u\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{i}+\tfrac{\partial_{x_{i}}p^{0}}{3T^{0}}\theta=\mu(T^{0})\partial_{\zeta}^{2}u_{i}+f_{i}\,,\\
&\rho^{0}\partial_{t}\theta+\rho^{0}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{x}}\theta+\rho^{0}(\partial_{x_{3}}\mathfrak{u}^{0}_{3}\zeta+u^{0}_{1.3})\partial_{\zeta}\theta+\tfrac{2}{3}\rho^{0}\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathfrak{u}^{0}\theta=\tfrac{3}{5}\kappa(T^{0})\partial_{\zeta}^{2}\theta+g\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where
(t,\bar{x},\zeta)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
